mr president, the storming of the capitol after the widespread misinformation about elections, a surge in radicalisation and extremism driven by conspiracies, and a stark rise in threats against journalists and politicians in the netherlands – even at night at their homes: while we watch the disastrous effects of the spread of hate and misinformation online slowly unfold and seep into real life, we often hear that platforms need to take responsibility. but the internet shouldn’t be in the hands of a few companies that profit from the large spread of hate and disinformation. it’s up to us as politicians to take responsibility. and after 20 years without updates, we now have the chance to fundamentally change the rules on the internet. let’s take back control from big tech and put it firmly back into the hands of the people. we need to break big tech’s power over our information before it erodes our democracy. but for some colleagues, it seems a step too far to make the necessary fundamental changes. yes, as the text stands there will be good improvements for users, and i’m happy to see that our proposals have been taken on board for strong notice and action procedures and a ban on manipulating people’s choices online with dark patterns. but a few months ago, when frances haugen was here, every group in this house tried to have the loudest voice on how we would rein in big tech, and look at where we are now. the deal that was struck in the committee on the internal market and consumer protection (imco) was warmly welcomed by the big tech lobby, and there’s a strong call from them to not vote for any amendments. and to my colleagues in the ppe, s&d and renew groups who want to hold on to this imco deal: profiting financially from the spread of hate and disinformation must stop. three simple solutions can change this fundamentally and protect our democracy. first of all, turn polarising, personalised algorithms based on clicks and interaction off by default. secondly, ban pervasive tracking for ads online to stop these manipulative business models. and if we as europe are serious about taking on big tech, let’s look at the need for a european platform supervisor, because strong rules are nothing without enforcement. these are proposals you can vote on today, and i call on you all to do so, because our democracy is too important to leave in the hands of large tech companies.
president, commissioners and colleagues, many platforms such as facebook are actually operating like a factory without windows at the moment. no policymaker, no social organisation, let alone the individual citizen, really knows exactly what is going on behind the walls of that factory. we're not getting a handle on the algorithms, and we're being pushed in a certain direction without knowing it. abuse is not being prevented or is being delayed, and there is no clear legal framework. everyone is treated on a bit of a random basis. and that's why it's high time we set a limit, set rules for tech companies. companies need to be more responsible about the content we want to put out. and we owe it to ourselves to take responsibility and to make a clear legislative framework. i have three children of my own, aged four, two and a newborn. the two oldest are already watching youtube videos a lot. vlad and niki will know the young parents. well, i'm honestly concerned about the moment they realize that they actually have unlimited access to all the content that comes online, and especially the content that's distributed through the major platforms. the control algorithms or the intrusive personalized advertising can be harmful to them, among other things. i am therefore proud of the decision we are about to take to better protect minors in the online world in the future. with a ban on commercial personalised advertising targeting children and increased transparency requirements, such as mandatory labelling of deepfakes, we are providing minors with a safe online environment. with the digital services act and the previously adopted digital markets act, we are going to set the gold standard worldwide. i am convinced that this will lead to a better, safer online environment where users' fundamental rights are protected and where companies are also clear about their rights and obligations. big tech will no longer be able to run its course without rules and without accountability. security over economic gain.
mr. chairman, please! i'm not going to let you get away with this. we cannot ignore the imperfections of the digital marketplace and their impact on our privacy, consumer rights, security, or freedom of public debate. digital platforms have created a situation where, whether they like it or not, they create reality, including political reality, without any responsibility for it. complicated internal rules and unclear procedures for appealing decisions, such as deleting content on social media, are examples of self-regulation that does not work. the digital services act is needed to address the lack of control and transparency. however, we must remember that the internet is not just a digital giant, but also a huge number of small and medium-sized businesses that can effectively reach customers through their online presence. their situation is specific and should not be subject to excessive burden and regulation.
mr president, i would like to thank you for your time. we are in the midst of a digital revolution that is fundamental to our daily lives. the analysis suggests that within three years up to a quarter of global economic activity will be digital. in a decade, that ratio will double. the digital market is therefore evolving at an ever-increasing pace and it is therefore important to develop not only digital infrastructure and digital skills but also digital legislation. this is key and in this respect the digital services act is such a landmark piece of legislation that we can speak of a year zero for digital rules. they are so important that, when set correctly, these european standards will be world standards. these rules must not only protect citizens, consumers, but should also help create new jobs, especially by small firms, which traditionally create the most jobs. not in the form of absolute prohibitions, restrictions or an increase in bureaucracy, but in the form of clear rules that will be functional and set clear rules and obligations for all participants. of course, there are many friction surfaces in such an important design. in particular, rules on the regulation of illegal content need to be carefully adjusted so that regulation does not become a tool of censorship. what's illegal in the offline world, must be illegal online. i am aware of the serious societal impact of disinformation on the development of society, and we need to create sufficient scope for a qualified assessment of individual requests to avoid the opposite effect. i also understand the concerns of the media from the multiple provisions, when they fear deterioration of the conditions for their work. but i would remind you that this is horizontal legislation, which is important and will continue to evolve. i also consider it crucial to protect our greatest asset, which is why i support the idea that advertising cannot be targeted at those who cannot yet give informed consent, namely our children. i believe that this proposal is really crucial. i thank all those who have contributed to it, and i believe that with this approval we will contribute to fairness, transparency and better protection of citizens in the digital world.
mr president, the digital services act aims to benefit eu citizens by providing safer and more transparent access to online platforms. the process towards an agreement in this house has, as expected, been a rather complex one. compromises reached will not necessarily provide an enduring answer to all pending questions. this legislation should therefore be seen as a crucial step of the work in progress. some issues that are technical and political at the same time, clearly, will need further consideration. for example, we stress the need for an ongoing audit of how digital service providers carry out their functions. but auditing firms of the accounting or legal kind – this traditional kind – cannot automatically be taken as competent or as qualified for the purposes of auditing under the dsa. should we not therefore have some definition of professional standards by which to ensure that auditors under the dsa regime are fit for purpose? furthermore, which precise role will financial and competition authorities be playing in the new mechanism? then there is a wider strategic question that needs to be faced. we are assuming all the time that regulation will be enough to deal with the rise of digital services and the associated risks and conditions that have come with them. but have digital service firms operating globally become too big for regulation to be able to really cope – as, for instance, in advertising and access to information? in which case, should we not work towards breaking up into smaller units the effective global monopolies that are outpacing and will continue to outpace regulation?
mr president, madam vice president, commissioner, christel, i have to tell you that i am not going to let you go. the digital services act is about taking away the digital giants' control over our data. but it's not about handing that power over to authoritarian governments and their institutions. so we're setting up a game-changing moderation policy, and i can hear clearly in this room that not everyone likes it. oh, my god. this regulation is about us being able to decide for ourselves what we want to see on the internet, about corporations not being able to drive fear for profit, as they are doing now, not creating a wave of hatred or spreading misinformation that can threaten health or even life. it's about not being followed without our explicit consent. it's about not allowing our sensitive data to be used to build trauma algorithms. to ruthlessly protect our youngest children from the ads that are following them. but it is also about ensuring fair competition in the market and giving european start-ups and small companies a chance against europe's tech giants. and that's why we're putting an incomparably greater burden on these big companies. and this is right and this is very important, because it is on these big companies that much more responsibility lies. the impact on the way our shared digital space looks is much greater on their side.
president, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the commission's proposal for a law on digital services is increasing the pressure to moderate even more and to remove harmful content from online platforms. big tech is fine with it as long as they can protect their business models. i have brought forward the following points as a co-negotiator: establishing definitions of what is harmful and hateful, because good legislation starts with clear definitions; restore the fundamental right to freedom of expression by also prohibiting the structural removal of legal content; the need for full transparency of the algorithms used; and promote competition for big tech by minimising rules for small online businesses. unfortunately, with insufficient success, and therefore i cannot vote in favour of this version of the digital services act. however, the amendments by ecr, id and the green colleague from the libe committee are an excellent improvement on the current proposal. ladies and gentlemen, in order to prevent the digital services act from becoming a digital censorship law, i would urge everyone to vote in favour of these amendments. that makes the digital services act significantly better, and including these amendments i recommend voting in favour.

the current toll system in the eu is fragmented and has many political sensitivities. the new eurovignette directive offers a common, ambitious and forward-looking european approach. we are taking a big step towards fairer and greener road transport. whoever pollutes the most, pays the most: this principle is essential to the achievement of our objectives under the green deal. the directive puts a co2 tax on trucks at the heart of it, because it allows us to further stimulate the transition to zero-emission trucks. in addition, the proceeds from the eurovignette are invested in modern and safe roads, and thus in the safety of all road users.
mr president, the courageous ukrainian people are standing up to an evil act of aggression. europe answers ukraine’s call for help. we rise up to defend those who are fighting for freedom and democracy. the eu is showing unity and we are finally showing that we are not just a soft power. we are crippling russia’s economy, isolating putin’s regime and reinforcing ukraine’s defence. but this is just the start and we have to do more by excluding more russian operators and banks from swift, by blocking the russian central bank’s use of western currencies – and we should even consider blocking russian ships in our harbours and more. also, we have to increase our support for ukraine’s economy and defence with money and weapons. my message to the ukrainian people: europe stands with you. slava ukraini!
president, commissioner ferreira, ladies and gentlemen, european cities and regions are facing enormous challenges. i thought first of all about making houses more sustainable, adapting infrastructure, sustainable transport, and of course not forgetting: covid-19 is out. look at the health care industry or the tourism industry. there is a lot to catch up with and the cohesion funds can help with that. so it's time we really start thinking about that and start implementing it. but of course, now more than ever, the flow of refugees from ukraine is topical. i am very pleased, commissioner ferreira, that you have promised to look at how the cohesion funds can contribute to this. because this crisis is the first one we have to face. it is therefore important that the member states contribute to this as soon as possible, and i am glad that you are playing a major role in this.
madam president, the european union's common agricultural policy is designed to ensure food security. and what will be the food security situation in a situation where we have the occupation of ukraine by russia, where thousands of refugees are fleeing from ukraine to europe, and where agricultural production has actually stopped in ukraine and russia? should we fear a food crisis? i'd take a few numbers. together with ukraine, russia accounts for more than 30% of world trade in wheat and barley, 17% of world trade in maize and more than 50% of world trade in sunflower oil. of course there will be some impact, especially in terms of global trade, but there is no immediate crisis for the european union and certainly this cannot be a reason for us to reduce our solidarity with the refugees from ukraine and with the people in ukraine. in any case, the cap and its reform is the right response to the climate challenge. what we can consider is moving some of the dates, but we shouldn't abandon it.
that concludes the item.
the sitting is closed and it will resume tomorrow, tuesday, 8 march, at 9.00 with the debate on the report by ms kalniete on foreign interference in all democratic processes in the european union, including disinformation. the agenda has been published and is available on the european parliament website.
i declare closed the 2021-2022 session of the european parliament.
i declare the 2022-2023 session of the european parliament open.
mr president, i would like to draw attention to rule 237a on extraordinary measures. according to this rule, parliament may, ‘in exceptional and unforeseeable circumstances beyond its control, make a temporary derogation from parliament’s usual procedures.’ scientists have said that the effect of the covid pass on transmission is close to zero, even though global data confirms that the pandemic has ended and we have now entered an endemic phase without serious risk to life and health. now that belgium and france return to normal, the time has come for the european parliament to also return to its normal and usual procedures. it doesn’t make any sense to think there is a normal world outside the parliament where we can get together in bars and restaurants without masks, without social distancing and without covid passes, but it’s too dangerous and unsafe within the parliament and that we still need these restrictions. dear president, there is no logic in continuing extraordinary measures, not even a tiny bit. let’s end these restrictions today.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, for too many years in europe we have underestimated the problem of foreign interference in our democracies and have not considered the disastrous consequences that would have had and which, unfortunately, have had, as the images of war in these days confirm. attempts at foreign interference are increasing worldwide and are becoming more sophisticated, in part thanks to the use of artificial intelligence. it is not always easy to identify them because they take on different forms and often also find allies and accomplices within the systems to be conditioned. we need to be aware of this risk and we need to make our communities more resilient and more ready to repel any form of attack. today, with the work of the inge committee, parliament wants to send a strong signal to citizens and institutions, but also to those countries which for years have taken advantage of our low guard to spread and amplify messages of hatred and disinformation, to feed fears and fragility, to delegitimize a system and weaken a model such as the european one. the european union is indeed a scary thing: our democracy, our standards of freedom and human rights, our economic prosperity are all things that frighten those countries that make coercion and fear the only means of controlling their populations. today we decide to be more vigilant, to be more vigilant and to start a process which will enable the european union and its member states to develop a coordinated strategy to combat foreign interference and to defend themselves against those who manipulate information to their own advantage and spread lies. we must defend our model and be a reference for other democratic countries outside the european union, so that they can protect themselves from external attacks and threats.
madam chairman! the fight against disinformation and fake news has never been more important. we are seeing just now how putin's propaganda machine is an active party in the war in ukraine. disinformation is a direct threat to our free and democratic world and has become the preferred tool of authoritarian regimes in undermining electoral outcomes, citizens' decisions and public trust. we must not turn a blind eye to this. that is why i welcome the many good proposals in the inge report. as rapporteur for the dsa, i particularly welcome the report's focus on the role of social media in the spread of disinformation. for too long, social media algorithms have been spreading hate, division and fake news. instead of removing or toning down problematic content and the accounts that spread it, social media has created an algorithmic megaphone that gets that content out to even more people. and at the same time, they've made money on this business model of spreading these malicious algorithms. this has to stop now. many of the good proposals in the report i am fighting to get into the final dsa legislation: the algorithms must be made transparent, the platforms must be accountable, we must have removed the 'dark patterns' and we must clearly label 'deep fake' and online advertising. we must ban advertising to minors and then we must look at the business models of the platforms, because they are partly responsible for the problem we have. better today than tomorrow.
madam president, i would like to speak to you about the nicts. these four letters were until recently the pride of our democracies. the new information and communication technologies enabled the greatest number of our citizens to be informed, to exchange information and to live. i say "allowed" because these four letters are now twisted, corrupted by democracies like china, venezuela and, of course, russia. these countries manipulate, falsify, and twist the truth. these countries are interfering with our democratic processes -- we saw it with the brexit referendum, the italian referendum in 2016, the french presidential election in 2017. they would even use it to finance political forces present here in this parliament. investigations are ongoing. if recent events push these forces - as we heard this morning - to sudden conversions, so much the better. the dragon heads of modern times are xi jinping, nicolas maduro and vladimir putin. they want the death of our democracies. for our elections, for political advertising, we must impose new rules, madam president, dear eva, with firmness. let's join forces to defeat these multi-headed dragons.
madam president, the report is very timely, and it has a wealth of recommendations. starting from our own institutions, we need to be much more aware that some meps have become channels of undue foreign influence. those travelling to authoritarian countries to observe fake elections should be sanctioned much more severely than is now the case, and the so-called friendship groups should be made much more transparent and accountable. the report also names and shames a number of members of political elites who have been captures in the service of authoritarian regimes, including two former finnish prime ministers, paavo lipponen and esko aho, who have both long worked to advance putin’s political and economic interests in europe. in hindsight, the warfare by russia against ukraine is a logical continuation of information warfare. we admire the courageous ukrainian resistance but, at the same time, in russia a distorted version of reality prevails. seventeen russian—speaking writers, among them nobel laureate svetlana alexievich, have started an appeal to all russian language speakers in the world to use all possible means of communication to contact directly their acquaintances and friends living in russia, telling them the truth about the war and about the tragic shelling of civilians. nobel laureate dmitry muratov stressed in a hearing last week in this parliament that there is another russia to the one represented by putin and his cronies. we must inspire the whole of society to combat the lies and disinformation.
what is the name of a government which is so concerned with what can be said and what cannot, what is true and what is not? of course, freedom of speech ends where preaching violence begins, but the survival of democracy requires debate, requires people to take a punch, requires people to be able to say what they think. the european union is committed to combating disinformation. a dangerous path, a path that leads to censorship. dutch 'fact-checkers' ticked people off when they said the european union was developing a qr passport. a little later, that "fake news" became reality. the fact-checkers were wrong. the lesson: caution is in order. of course, we are also against russian propaganda, but we must be the ones to stand up for freedom of expression. the european union is in danger of becoming the very thing it is trying to combat! a government that interferes too much with what citizens say and whether that's true, that's a government i call communist. our beautiful, democratic europe deserves better than this.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – madam president, at our borders, putin is putting the lives of millions of people at risk. his brutal invasion targets the citizens of ukraine violating international humanitarian law, and people are desperately seeking protection. it is unprecedented how quickly the number of refugees keeps growing, and the eu’s response is unprecedented as well, with the activation of the temporary protection directive. we applaud the commission and council for the swift and united response to give their full support to the refugees and their families. it will only work, however, if every member state is ready to host a substantial number of refugees. so let the weakest part of the directive, namely the voluntariness, not become the obstacle for an effective and humanitarian response. coordination, funding and guidance by the commission will be much needed. it is encouraging that the council includes refugees who lived in ukraine, but all people fleeing ukraine must be safe. referring third—country nationals, even if they lived permanently in ukraine, to their region of origin, creates huge insecurities and risks, and in our view, this is unjustified, so please do not make any distinction. and this also goes for the border. we hear and see discrimination on skin colour, ethnic background, gender and sexual identity. we need agencies and international organisations to ensure equal treatment and to take care for the vulnerable people. the heartening hospitality of citizens will not be sufficient with the rising numbers and vulnerabilities, and the commissioner already also referred to the risk of trafficking for minors. member states should therefore organise a safe route to hosting locations. and zelenskyy was right when he called the transport of refugees to belarus and russia immoral, because who wants to find refuge in a country of your own invader? so we must help them, but also the russian citizens under pressure to find protection in our countries. refugees from ukraine deserve our unconditional support, no matter how long, no matter how many. but let me be very clear. being forced to flee means the same misery, despair and fear wherever you are in the world. let this war at our doorstep not distract us from the needs and rights of refugees from other countries. do not believe politicians who make us believe that protection for the one means less protection for the other. if we equally share our global responsibility, we can offer every refugee the protection that she or he needs and deserves.
mr president, barbaric scenes are unfolding as putin’s illegal invasion is escalating. terrible violence against innocent ukrainians is on full display at our own borders. people are fleeing from burning cities. it is without question that we need to give them a safe place. and europe is taking responsibility. we see countless initiatives of eu citizens to show solidarity. it is our duty to organise this well. that means full financial and operational support to member states at the borders of ukraine, also by participation of eu agencies like easo and frontex. members states need to show solidarity too, by reallocation of refugees. we have to set an example in organising a safe place for refugees in our own region. the eu is united and together we will manage this for the safety of the people of ukraine.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, europe’s response to the russian aggression was almost united – almost. while the rest of europe was discussing sanctions, bosnian serb leader dodik talked with the russian ambassador and with minister lavrov. this continuous flirting with putin reconfirms his disinterest in democracy, international order and european security. yet the brutal invasion in ukraine shows the need to more strongly combat russian interference in the balkans, as it directly threatens european security. the long—overdue sanctions for dodik’s secessionist aggression must be imposed right away, including financial ones. so thanks, commissioner, for your clear position on that; it’s a first step. bosnian citizens have become truly scared, reliving their traumas from the 1990s. russian influence and interference also moves them further away from the eu perspective, which is devastating for their right to live in a stable and fully—fledged democracy. yet strong support for bosnian civil society and the eu integration process right now would really increase their trust in the eu and in a democratic and prosperous future. let us strongly engage with the citizens, with civil society and, as a first step, ensure free and fair elections in october. that would be the best and most promising medicine against russian interference.
mr president, let me be very clear: we need to get rid of the russian gas. it is not only undesirable, but even immoral to continue to plunder putin's coffers in this day and age. and those who think that the prices have only just been raised by sanctions, make no mistake: the prices were already high. putin's been rigging gas prices for a while. we must get rid of such manipulative practices as soon as possible. how are we gonna do that? we have seen a wonderful plan today, repowereu, a realistic plan to reduce gas from russia by two thirds this year. and let us not forget: one of the best solutions we have already exists, and it's called the european green deal, our medium-term plan. in addition, there are certainly still large gains to be made in the field of energy saving. energy you don't consume, you don't have to import. for example, the demand for gas can be reduced by about ten billion cubic meters by lowering the thermostat by one degree. let energy efficiency and energy conservation be acts of resistance against russian aggression. it's time to act. let us not allow our households, vulnerable groups and small and mediumsized enterprises to suffer the consequences of the high energy prices imposed by russia. and that starts with strategic independence.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your reply. millions of europeans are thinking twice these days about whether they can still fill their cars with petrol or turn up the heat. consumer prices for oil and gas are at an all-time high. yes, the situation on the world market is tense, no question. the bitter truth is that the government is making energy expensive. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's energy policy is not undermined by the crisis. taxes and levies account for almost 70%. the state is shamelessly enriching itself at the expense of its citizens in this energy crisis. the green tax and carbon pricing are imposing a huge burden on low-income households. they're highly antisocial. energy, ladies and gentlemen, must remain affordable for all. i therefore call on the german government to abolish the absurd and harmful eco-tax and the carbon tax immediately. the eu commission is now obliged to include oil and gas in the list for reduced vat, up to a zero rate, in order to allow member states a significant reduction and to cushion the effects of the crisis on the world market for the people.
president, commissioner, the political attention paid to making our energy system cleaner has been justifiably high in recent years. but at the same time, we have left too much to the market in terms of affordability and security of supply. and we see that in the energy bill, in the pump, and in our dependence on russia. it is morally unacceptable for me, in these dark days on our continent, that we are contributing 600 million euros a day to putin's war by importing gas and oil. we need to reduce that dependence quickly. but it's not easy. that is why we need realpolitik in our energy policy. question to countries that wanted to close nuclear plants: keep them open. demand from gas producing countries: give us more, and ask households and businesses to turn down the heat. let us accelerate solar, wind and hydrogen projects and better connect the energy systems of the european countries. europe has awoken from its geopolitical and strategic hibernation. we must be much more capable of generating our own energy, of defending our own european territory and of producing essential products such as medicines, batteries and computer chips ourselves. it's really time we start taking responsibility for our place in the world again.
president, commissioner, with the energy transition the european union has made itself completely dependent on russian gas. i have already mentioned that commissioner timmermans' climate plans are an irresponsible experiment with our economy. with the russian attack on ukraine, that prediction has quickly come true. yet commissioner timmermans said yesterday that we still need to meet the previously set climate target of 55 percent less co2 emissions by 2030. in fact, he says that goal is the solution, not the problem! but energy prices are exploding and a deep recession is looming. life is becoming priceless. not the energy transition, but the supply of affordable and reliable energy must now be a top priority. i therefore call on the commission: stop the green deal and focus on affordable and reliable energy. the only solution is to reopen immediately closed nuclear power stations, to immediately start using closed or downsized coal plants, to immediately start using our own gas fields. because necessity breaks the law.
president, vera, why do you keep worrying about women's rights? there's a war going on in europe. this is a question i get asked more and more often, and in fact always, when it comes to gender equality. at the moment, european women and men are fighting in ukraine for their country, trying to save their children and bring them to safety. some women are even forced to give birth in the subway. in any crisis, whether it's war or a pandemic, it's women who are hit hardest. every time. the numbers speak for themselves. but when it comes to the strategic choice of a safe life for you and your family, women are either not at the table or are severely underrepresented. the number of women active in politics is still uneven in all eu member states. female politicians are more often the target of harassment, especially on social media. it discourages them from taking an active part in politics and participating in democratic decision-making. in my own country, the netherlands, that equality is also sad. there's a local election next week. on average, three out of ten councillors are women, and that's in 2022! this has to be different. it is in the direct interest of our democracy that people be represented equally. here in the european parliament and beyond.
the commission has already made a number of proposals. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. let me make one thing clear: what the russian regime and putin have done here, namely to wage a war of aggression, where there is also a blood toll among the civilian population, must be condemned in every form. war can never be a solution. but the way this house has reacted to this with a resolution is also, for me, throwing oil on the fire and the wrong way. i would like to illustrate this with a few examples. the european union has a duty to help the countries of the region to find a way out of the crisis. another example: bringing ukraine into the european union by express procedure is also the wrong way to go. how many of the european criteria do you want to throw out? you have thrown dublin overboard, you have thrown schengen overboard, you have thrown maastricht overboard and now copenhagen with the copenhagen criteria. does this european framework no longer count at all? and the third case is the history of sanctions, where, in my view, the leyens and bidens and the heads of state are not taking much damage here. no, it's the people outside who have to pay over 2 euros, sometimes 2.50 euros for the litre of fuel. i'm all for doing everything we can to de-escalate. i am in favour of a proposal by ukraine, which is now on the table, to declare itself neutral, as a proper way forward. ... (the president withdraws the floor from the speaker.)
president, madam prime minister, mr high representative, the war in ukraine has put us under a very heavy burden. war in europe? that was a thing of the past. invest in defense? all that was no longer necessary, found a lot of parties. we know better now. the rudder must be turned, that much is clear. no, not by creating a new european command structure, let alone a european army, but by military cooperation and coordination between member states. for our defense, we already have an excellent partnership. that is nato. let us cherish that and above all invest in it. it is also up to the member states to take their responsibility. we need money. in fact, it takes a lot of money! germany has finally woken up with its 100 billion euro recovery operation. but us? where's the rest of it? the nato standard of 2% of gdp is not something we have for nothing. russia is a great danger to world peace. we must guard against that.
madam president, mr commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. the recent crises that have hit the world and the european union have exposed a number of problems that were previously under-recognised. not only did we wake up to a new world after this war started, but we also saw things again. we can now see more clearly all the manipulations, lies, deceits and crimes of the great dictator. despite the enormous fears and challenges of war, we still need to pay sufficient attention to environmental challenges. we cannot postpone the problems of the future because we are running out of time. this will require many changes in our conduct and way of life. we have developed an over-dependence on other countries in many important areas, from energy, to the lack of ingredients for medicines and components for modern technologies, not to mention food security. batteries are a key technology for europe's energy transition. so we need a new strategic approach to their production, their use, and even their decomposition. in addition to the positive aspects, batteries also have a detrimental impact on the environment. our dependence on scarce natural resources in various fields requires that we be even more careful with what we have. the circular economy for sustainable development and the reuse of strategic materials are a hugely important part of our future. by setting and respecting sustainability criteria and with the cooperation of producers and users, while respecting the relevant legislation, we will take steps in the right direction.
mr president, commissioner, the eu urgently needs a new battery regulation as the electrification of transport progresses at a rapid pace and as the production and demand for batteries increase. i am pleased that parliament's report sends a strong signal to promote sustainable battery production in europe, which is crucial both for investment and security of supply and for the reduction of emissions targets. the increasing demand for batteries is putting pressure on the availability of raw materials. in view of the growing demand, it is important to ensure the responsible production of batteries, which is balanced in the parliament's report. it is also positive that the report emphasizes the use and development of new innovative raw materials in battery production. wood-based lignin is a good example of such a new, sustainable material that contributes to sustainability and the circular economy of the battery industry. new forms of transport, such as electric bicycles and electric scooters, have exploded in recent times. it is good that the ambitious recycling targets of the regulation have been extended to include batteries for light transport in order to ensure efficient and large-scale recycling in the sector. in addition, the flexibility for producers and producer organisations regarding battery waste streams ensures that already efficient recycling schemes will also be able to move easily under this new regulation. the new battery regulation will create sustainable growth and new investment in europe.
president. - thank you for your report. when we talk in this parliament about the circular economy, the farmer-to-the-table strategy or the green deal, the targets often cannot be high enough. i think it is important to have a realistic policy. policies that promote food security rather than undermine it. a policy that allows my constituents to pay their energy bills and have money left over at the end of the month. policies that put the citizen at the centre instead of the bureaucratic plans of the european union. i often have to vote against bills. proposals that do not make the lives of citizens better, but much more expensive. today, however, things are different. the report we are discussing today deals with the sustainability of batteries, among other things, and i intend to vote in favour of that bill. not because i think climate change is necessary. in fact, i think it is an exercise which is going to cost european citizens a lot of money and hit our economies very hard. but if the eu wants that transition to be necessary, then let it not cause additional environmental damage in other parts of the world. and under no circumstances should child labour be used to bring about the transition. for these reasons, i shall vote in favour of the bill.
madam president, in 2021 in mexico many journalists were killed, but also 42 human rights defenders, of whom 30 were defenders of the rights of the original peoples on their ancestral lands. obviously, mexico must improve its inadequate mechanism for the protection of human rights defenders and journalists, but the union must also be fully committed to this endeavour. we need, first, the european union delegations in countries like mexico to be involved in the effective implementation of the council guidelines for the protection of human rights defenders. and secondly, the union cannot accept the projects of large european companies in mexico and the rest of latin america which violate the rights of indigenous peoples, which degrade the environment, which damage labour rights, which undermine the rule of law, including the involvement of organised crime, or which establish a neo-colonial economic structure. the future work in this parliament on the directive on due diligence is therefore of great importance and must take all these objectives into account.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i would also like to support the motion for a resolution on this point. i think it's extremely important to respond to the situation in mexico. this is all the more important because mexico is our key partner in latin america. it's a long-standing business partner, a political partner, a major country, and the situation has been getting worse recently in mexico with respect to human rights defenders and journalists. it's been said right here, there's no point in repeating the facts, the facts are truly tragic. six journalists have been killed in the first half of this year alone, and the situation is unfortunately getting worse. we must ask the president, who is one of the causes of this unhappy state, that the state, the political representation, begin to address the situation, that the justice system begin to investigate these cases, because most of these murders are not investigated. and we need to connect that to our dialogue between the european union and mexico and we need to connect that to mutually beneficial trade. it is not possible to do business with a country where journalists and human rights defenders are subjected to such violence.
madam president, i would like to thank you. maría de lourdes maldonado lópez, margarito martínez, josé luis gamboa, ever lópez vázquez, roberto toledo just to name some of the journalists and media workers who have been murdered in mexico in recent years. mexico is the most dangerous place in the world for journalists, except for the world's war zones. since 2018, the situation has deteriorated. in total, 47 journalists have been murdered. journalists who investigate corruption by officials or drug cartels live in particular peril. since taking office in 2018, mexican president lópez obrador has humiliated and threatened independent journalists in his daily press conferences. under the pretext of countering fake news, the government has set up a state media platform to attack investigative journalism. in total, at least 68 human rights activists have been murdered. violence against women is widespread and femicide is common. environmental, indigenous and women's rights activists are particularly vulnerable. the mexican government must take concrete measures to ensure that journalists can work without fear of reprisals. the eu and its member states now have a special responsibility to put the issue at the top of the agenda in their dialogues with mexico.
aachtaráin, tá bród orm labhairt as gaeilge is a debate that has been going on for some time now. mexico is the place in the world where there are no foreigners, outside of official war zones. the courses are held in august and october, and the participants are invited to attend the courses in august. you're gonna make it worse, and nobody's gonna want to get in your way. they were not pioneers of 90% of the irish and mexican immigrants. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament and the council adopt a common position on the proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work. the mexican authorities have indicated that an alien has been arrested. we are acting on behalf of mexico to fulfill its obligations and to take appropriate action to ensure that our citizens are protected and kept safe.
madam president, the growing stigmatisation, the weakening of institutions, the increase in violence and the diffuse impunity all provide a clear message: in mexico, the press and freedom of expression overall are under siege. since his election in 2018, lópez obrador has not only failed to improve the country's human rights record, but he has allowed the loss of many of the hard-fought gains activists, rights groups and campaigners have achieved since the end of one-party rule in mexico. the western world, the european union included, has been noticeably silent regarding these accelerating attacks on democracy and fundamental rights. so far, the democratic clause of the global agreement has not resulted in any decisions or measures that help alleviate the continuous violation of human rights. if journalists and activists, especially activists for indigenous people’s human rights, are deliberately targeted and threatened while those who attacked them go unpunished, democracy itself is also under threat. as we are working at the modernisation of our joint comprehensive agreement, we need to be clear with the representatives – mexico is a long-standing and, we stress in our resolution, vital partner of the eu – that the prospect of ratification is heavily reliant on improvement on human rights and on the government's clear commitment to international standards and obligation. ¡derechos y libertad para todos los periodistas y los activistas mexicanos! ¡no estáis solos!
vice-president of the commission. – madam president, honourable members, journalists and human rights defenders are central to functioning democracies. they are key in holding governments and societies to account and speaking up for those who otherwise would not have a voice. mexico is one of the eu’s strategic partners and a like-minded partner in multilateral fora, and currently we extend our gratitude and appreciation for mexico’s support in condemning russian aggression and standing up for ukraine’s sovereignty and its democracy at the un security council, the general assembly and the human rights council. the protection of human rights and upholding the rule of law and accountability are part of the government’s declared political objectives, but mexico continues to face serious challenges in the implementation of its human rights legal framework. the most significant threat to the promotion and protection of human rights remains organised crime. the current situation as regards journalists is especially worrying. we are highly concerned about the rising number of assassinations and attacks against journalists and human rights defenders in mexico and about the hostile environment in which they work and risk their lives. the press can never be fully free if journalists are facing harassment, attacks or even mortal danger while doing their jobs. mexico’s federal mechanism for the protection of journalists and human rights defenders, launched in 2012, is an interesting model. however, the death toll suggests that a far more comprehensive effort is needed if the challenge of violence against journalists is to be successfully addressed. we regularly address these issues with the mexican authorities with regard to the human rights situation, as well as specific cases of human rights defenders and journalists, both in a dedicated human rights dialogue and in other contexts. as well as speaking out clearly against this violence, we engage continuously through our delegation in mexico city with civil society, human rights defenders, journalists and international actors. this is in order to show our support and to raise awareness about the need for further action to address the current situation. we also provide practical, financial and technical support to the largest extent possible. governments can never be fully accountable if human rights defenders and journalists cannot carry out their work safely. attacks against journalists and human rights defenders are therefore a threat to the foundation of our democracies. when they come under attack, effective protection must be provided, and the perpetrators of such attacks must be brought to justice. the fight against impunity is therefore a key priority to stop threats and attacks against journalists. if not addressed, the silencing of journalists and human rights defenders is bound to continue. we welcome the european parliament’s strong commitment on this issue and reiterate the eu’s commitment to cooperating and working with mexico to foster the protection of human rights defenders and journalists, both with concrete projects on the ground and with dialogue, including our regular high-level dialogues on human rights and civil society seminars.
the debate is closed. the vote will take place today.
the next item is the debate on six motions for resolutions on myanmar, one year after the coup (2022/2581(rsp))*. _______________* see minutes.
author. – madam president, one year on from the coup in myanmar, the situation there remains critical. we continue to condemn in the strongest possible terms the coup executed by the military in myanmar in february of last year and call for the reinstatement of the elected government. since they seized power, the military have implemented a series of severe restrictions on freedoms and everyday life. they have shown little respect for human rights and are causing untold suffering for the people of myanmar. particularly concerning are reports of killings and unlawful arrests of peaceful pro-democracy protesters. the office of the un high commissioner for human rights estimates that at least 1 500 people have been killed and over 11 000 arrested since the military seized power last year. the humanitarian situation in myanmar is dire, with reports of a systematic and sustained attacks on the rohingya, christians and other groups perpetrated by the military government. with regard to everyday life, the government has imposed a number of extreme restrictions on personal freedoms, including freedom of expression and access to media and information. women’s rights also have been seriously curtailed since the military took power, with the women subject to widespread detention, sexual assault, gender-based violence and in some cases killings. the government has also exploited the covid-19 pandemic to facilitate a crackdown on pro-democracy activists, a trend which is extremely concerning. it is imperative that former leaders – the president and the state counsellor – are immediately and unconditionally released and the results of the democratic elections of november 2020 are respected. although myanmar’s former government did not demonstrate full respect for human rights either, they represented the only viable alternative to the current military rule. it is crucial, therefore, that we, the international community, do everything in our power to halt the systematic human rights abuses that are occurring in myanmar.
author. – madam president, one year after the military coup of 1 february 2021, we can see that the situation is very dire and that we need to add to the pressure against the military junta. we as parliament welcome sanctions which have now come to four rounds, but we would demand that more needs to be done as for sanctions. we can still add, for instance, the state administrative council to the list as an entity, not just as a person. we welcome the fact that now, finally, the council has added the national oil and gas company, moge, to the sanctions. but we demand that a derogation allowing european oil and gas companies to operate and conduct transactions in the country be removed. so what more can be done? i believe that it's very important to point out to european businesses that they should not mix with anything in the business of myanmar which touches the interests and adds to the interests of the army, the tatmadaw. and finally, i want to say that we need to find support for the refugees fleeing the country. we especially need to expect that there will be no forced returns to thailand, china and india as neighbouring countries.
the war in ukraine demands almost all our attention. we'd almost forget that other parts of the world have terrible things going on. as in myanmar, where a coup d'état took place over a year ago and violently crushed the still fragile democracy. the sad balance after one year: more than 7 000 targeted attacks on civilians by the military; nearly 1 500 people were killed, including women and children; hundreds of thousands of people fleeing their own country; dozens of documented reports of attacks on churches and other religious institutions; religious leaders have been killed or arrested; the military is blocking the entry of humanitarian aid; there 's a shortage of everything ... fortunately, the council introduced a new round of sanctions earlier this year. i urge the council to continue this and, together with the international community, to increase pressure on the military junta to stop the violence and to stop blocking the delivery of humanitarian aid. the government of national unity, as the legitimate representative of the democratic aspirations of the people of myanmar, should also be involved in an inclusive political dialogue and in the search for a peaceful solution to the crisis. the people of myanmar must also be able to count on us!
madam president, armenia and azerbaijan agreed to a cessation of hostilities, but are still a long way from peace. the destruction of the cultural heritage in nagorno-karabakh, known as the republic of artsakh, is a clear example of this. the long-running conflict has had a catastrophic impact on the cultural heritage of the region. azerbaijan needs to actively protect the armenian cultural heritage in and around the republic of artsakh and stop its destruction. it's our duty to ensure the transmission to future generations of the cultural and natural heritage. no development can be sustainable without a strong cultural component. indeed, only an approach to development based on mutual respect and open dialogue among cultures can lead to lasting peace. i urge the european parliament and the european commission to use its powers and influence to prevent and punish acts of vandalism and desecration affecting armenian cultural heritage in nagorno-karabakh.
madam president, the reality of today shows us that history, identity, culture and religions matter. manipulation, falsification and destruction of historical heritage leads to hate, discrimination and violence. the war of putin against ukraine proves this one more time. he's also using these ugly instruments of history manipulation for justification of these terrible actions. reconciliation can be achieved only by reaching mutual trust. the topic of the destruction of cultural heritage in nagorno-karabakh against armenians is just one example of how important is trust. the destruction of any cultural heritage sites, artefacts and objects contributes to the escalation of hostilities, lack of mutual understanding and prejudice between and within societies. on the contrary, the respect of minorities, which is one of our core values, includes the protection of their cultural identity and heritage. the urgency resolution which we are voting today welcomes the unesco proposal to send an independent experts’ mission to visit nagorno-karabakh and assess the situation on the ground. we request further that international experts be given unhindered access by azerbaijan to all cultural sites for a proper inventory to be carried out. it is critical to remember that the protection, rebuilding and restoration of historical heritage and religious heritage should be seen in the broader framework of the conflict resolution between armenia and azerbaijan, and needs to be requested, supported and welcome. this process includes also the condemnation of any destructions of cultural, religious and historical sites, regardless of whether this happened in the past or in the present day, or by whom. the new generations need to have a peaceful and secure future without the hate from the past.
president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, armenia has a very rich history. it was the first country in the world to declare christianity the national religion in the fourth century after christ. many thousands of sites, including in nagorno-karabakh, are reminders of this. the long-running conflict with azerbaijan is now putting this cultural and religious heritage in great danger. many monuments have been damaged or destroyed. armenian traces are being erased by albanization of cultural heritage. it is a direct attempt to falsify history! with our joint resolution, we make a clear statement today: 1) the eu should address azerbaijan firmly for the systematic destruction of its cultural heritage; (2) an independent investigation mission is necessary to take an inventory of the damage on the spot; the european union must not allow the soviet union to continue to be a party to the conflict. the destruction of cultural and religious heritage in nagorno-karabakh must stop!

on behalf of the ppe group. – madam president, i would like to congratulate the rapporteur. the market stability reserve, in place since 2019, as executive vice-president timmermans has just reminded us, addresses the structural imbalance between the supply of and demand for allowances on the market, with the aim to stabilise the market by removing surplus allowance from it. the reserve functions by triggering adjustments to the annual auctions volumes. last year, as part of the fit for 55 package, the commission proposed also the first review of the ets and market stability reserve (msr) following an impact assessment. i would like to reiterate that the assessment showed the msr should be adapted to avoid shocks and to ensure complementarity, coherence and effectiveness in achieving the 2030 and 2050 climate ambition. it also revealed that the 12% intake rate would not be enough to ensure that the objectives of the msr in terms of reducing the surplus and ensuring market resilience will still be fulfilled. it is only fair to continue with the doubled intake rate for a few years and ensure that any review and adjustment of the msr intake rate in the future should aim to minimise regulatory complexity and market speculation, while ensuring a maximum degree of market predictability and that the eu ets is fit for purpose in line with the union’s increased climate ambitions for 2030. this is the reason why the epp has tabled this amendment on this matter. while the overall revision of the msr decision is part of the ets revision, this file was due to have a on behalf of the s&d group. – madam president, the market stability reserve (msr) might seem like a technicality, but it is immensely important for climate. it is not only the first file in the fit for 55 package, but also key to make the eu emissions trading system (ets) fit for purpose. the historical problem with the eu ets has been the high surplus of allowances after the euro and financial crisis. this has resulted in low prices, weak signals for industry to decarbonise. but the creation of the msr and also the swedish proposal in the last reform actually saved the european union from – if we count from my home country, sweden – 50 years of swedish emissions. this is probably the main reason why the ets prices are finally sending the right signal to the industry. and this is why we also now see a new green industrial revolution with new innovations such as green hydrogen popping up across europe. however, after the pandemic, we now see a new large surplus: 30 years of swedish emissions, if we should use my home country again to show you the picture. weakening the msr at this stage would be a huge mistake. today, i hope for a very broad support for a continuously strong msr without limiting further ambition in the ets negotiations. i want to thank my s&d colleague and rapporteur for good work and wish you the very best for the trilogues that we have ahead of us.

madam president, the criminal invasion of ukraine by russia will probably accelerate the geopolitical alignment between china and russia which has been going on for a while. obviously, china is not celebrating the invasion, but neither has it openly condemned it. china has sided with russia’s false arguments, has refused to join the sanctions, and we suspect they might help putin dodge them. there’s a strategic alliance between big authoritarian regimes – as imbalanced as it may be – because russia needs china much more than china needs russia. at the end of the day, it’s an alliance to better counter our strong relations with our like-minded partners. how should we europeans react to this? president xi calls for an independent eu approach toward china. i rather think we need to have an approach which, taking into account our significant trade and economic ties, also preserves our strategic interests and values and, together with our partners, ensures human rights and international law. last week’s summit was an excellent opportunity to display such an approach, but as usual when it comes to china, i am not sure that we fully took advantage of that opportunity.
president, high representative, last week's eu-china summit reminded me of the previous summit in 2020. at that time, merkel and macron thought they could conclude an investment agreement in a short time. the summit was a symbol of the eu's ambivalent attitude towards china: nice words about human rights, but in the end, the wallet is more important. the tone of last week was markedly different, but i still saw ambivalence again. yes, you spoke about ukraine, and rightly so. but you also decided to set up a working party to strengthen trade relations and to recognise each other's protected geographical indications. i do not understand this, because there is no prospect of restoring democracy in hong kong, restoring freedom of religion for christians in china, restoring freedom for the million uyghurs in concentration camps, while china still has sanctions against members of the european parliament. if china continues to trample on human rights like this, there can be no talk of enhanced economic relations. that should be clear.
madam president, i promise you i’ll stay on time. yesterday, the eu launched a fifth package of sanctions against putin and his allies. we strongly welcome this and the establishment of the eu and ukraine joint investigation team. we have to collect evidence and investigate war crimes and crimes against humanity in ukraine, as we have witnessed in bucha and other places. but the question is: is this enough? and the short answer is no. we cannot continue doing business because that is what we’re doing by importing russian gas, knowing about the atrocities that have happened in ukraine. we, as the european parliament, need to send a clear signal and support a total ban on fossil fuels from russia and hit putin where it hurts. we need this total ban on oil and gas imports to be effective immediately. the sooner the better. as president michel said, we need this probably sooner rather than later. well, are we going to wait until putin decides to cut us off? he already asks us to pay in roubles and we see that he is dividing us. some member states have cancelled all imports and others are really considering paying in roubles. we need to stop this. better to be one step ahead than in panic mode. and the good thing is that we have a plan to get off fossil fuels. we have repowereu and our european green deal. we can accelerate the process by delivering on the fit for 55 package and accelerating energy efficiency and deployment of renewables. we in the s&d group are ready for this. who would have thought that the european green deal would become an act of resistance against putin’s hostilities?
madam president, high representative, commissioner, dear colleagues, after his failed attempt to turn ukraine away from europe, putin finds himself haunted by a question: what must russia do with ukraine? and his answer? i quote: ‘we do not need a nazi, banderite ukraine which is an enemy of russia and a tool of the west used to destroy russia’. these lines are taken from an article by ria novosti, an official mouthpiece of the russian state, in response to the western sanctions to the massacre of civilians in bucha. does anyone in this chamber who is in their right mind, question the existence of russian fascism and chauvinism? the russian regime is an enemy of the west – all of us. dear colleagues, western support for ukraine must be based on the binary principle that either we are with ukraine or we are against freedom, democracy and all the values that we cherish in the european union. we must not even think about our discomfort or the disruption of our daily lives. we must do everything that is necessary to support the existential fight of ukraine. i regret that the fifth eu sanctions package does not cover all fossil fuels. it really is a shame on us. we are not convincing ukrainians that the european union is doing all it can and must. finally, high representative, i call on the european union to appoint a special representative for ukraine in order to properly coordinate the delivery of all kinds of aid and the eventual reconstruction process. let us get ready for this. we have to realise that the conference on the future of europe, without the people of ukraine, will never fulfil its main purpose. let’s invite ukrainians.
madam president, mr high representative, madam president of the commission, the world is becoming increasingly aware of putin's atrocities in ukraine. my first words are to the victims of bucha and their families and loved ones. as president zelensky said yesterday before the congress of deputies in spain, the massacres are too reminiscent of the massacre suffered by the spaniards in guernica in 1937: they are war crimes that must be thoroughly investigated, and the international criminal court must play its part. the crimes of putin, who torpedoes diplomatic negotiations, unfortunately present us with a prospect of a prolongation of the conflict, and in the face of this prospect europe must remain united - that is the most important thing - by supporting the ukrainians on all fronts, and also by sanctions - by better implementing them, because sanctions have a lack of implementation - and the greens are also supporting him with a total embargo of all fossil fuels from russia, including gas, and also uranium. but in the short term we must also look at cushioning the costs of war on our citizens so that we can be better prepared, and i want to welcome the decisions taken in the council on energy, which have allowed the price of gas to be limited, the extraordinary profits of the electricity companies to be taxed... that is important: we cannot make the usual people pay for these costs. and in the long term, as europeans, we have to reflect on what all these years of external dependence on fossil fuels have meant, which today are not only a problem for climate change, they are a threat to peace and security; we know this today: what is the point, therefore, in this context, of continuing, for example, to keep gas in the taxonomy? we must devote the maximum effort to the urgent deployment of renewables, put all our resources there; we will probably need a new european fund to be able to make that transition much faster. we have no time, because the ipcc report shows that we are running out of time to combat climate change.
the commission has already made a number of proposals. first of all, what putin is doing here is unacceptable in any form and condemnable in any form. as a representative of a party which many years ago also had a so-called memorandum of understanding with the united russia party, which served cultural exchange and economic revival, i can only say today: that friendship is over. it is unacceptable what is happening here on putin's part. the question is, how do i respond to it? i am not going to go into detail about the situation in the middle east. i think that among those who will not be affected by this sanctions policy, is russian president putin, who will not care about the energy price. i also think that commission president von der leyen will not be affected if she flies by private jet from vienna to bratislava or something like that. i don't think the president of the united states is going to be affected by that if he's going to be traveling in the president of the united states' vehicle, the beast, or air force one. but those who are really affected, and to a large extent, are the broad masses of people outside who are no longer pushing the price of energy, who are no longer pushing a price of more than 2 euros per litre of fuel, who are no longer pushing the high gas prices and so on and so forth. so it's the wrong way, because it's a shot in the knee. the question is what should you do instead? the answer to this question is, in my view, simple: all that de-escalates. it doesn't help if leyen flies to kiev, or the austrian chancellor who said to fly to kiev for a pr campaign. no, it would make sense if we did all the things that bring peace back. it would be a good idea if, for example, in austria, which is committed to neutrality, a tripolar peace conference were to begin - but without the americans, who have their own interests - and the ukrainians, europeans and russians here doing everything to put together a package that would secure peace and also serve the people outside.
president, high representative, ladies and gentlemen, i would also like to address my words to mrs von der leyen and mr michel, but it is clear time and again that they prefer not to listen to the elected representatives of the people. - i'll see you. the war in ukraine is threatening to escalate into a continental crisis. we've all seen the latest horrific footage. the whole of history shows that the russian military behaves in a barbaric way against the civilian population. murder, rape, and looting. every war again. we must continue to support ukraine. for europe, the lesson is 'we can't count on russia' but the harsh reality is that over the last decade we have only become more dependent on russian gas. the climate policy of this parliament - your policy - has filled putin's coffers to wage this war. we must reduce that dependence as quickly as possible. we cannot do that by relying on even more unreliable solar and wind energy, the very energy sources that have made us dependent on russia, because only gas can back up if there is no sun and no wind. instead, we must now fully commit to clean nuclear energy. france has already proved that this is the solution. and we need to invest heavily in innovation. then the short term. already, inflation is in the double digits. without affordable and reliable energy, we are facing a crisis of unprecedented proportions, and we must prevent it. the green deal, including the 'farmer to plate' strategy, must be scrapped. food security and affordable and reliable energy must now be top priorities. we can and we must use our own gas and fully exploit existing fossil and nuclear power stations. it's the only way to avert a catastrophe. and as far as ukraine is concerned, this war must end. that requires great political skill and not economic suicide. in the end, that is all that has served ukraine and europe. let us continue to work towards this goal with one voice.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – madam president, (inaudible) the united solidarity with refugees from ukraine is impressive. it reassures us that the european values and the right to protection do have a meaning in the european union, but these principles leave no room for double standards. people fleeing a war, persecution or inhuman treatment all fall under the same protection regime. the situation people are fleeing from determines their right to protection, not the country that they left. yet, despite the hospitality towards refugees from ukraine, the right to enter an asylum procedure is being violated at many european borders. at the polish borders, we see that in one place, thousands of people are welcomed, while only a few kilometres away, refugees are being pushed back into belarus, deprived of their rights, goods and dignity. in greece, the push-backs have become a daily and systematic practice. if a refugee manages to apply for asylum, the greek authorities refer them back to turkey, even when they know that they are not allowed to re-enter turkey. greece is an important country of entrance for refugees from syria, afghanistan and other dangerous countries. but the policies of greece simply mean that they all end up in a lawless situation. their lives and health are at risk, despite all the safeguards that we have in our legislation. the commission knows exactly what’s going on; many investigations conclude the same. so how is it possible that the commission accepts such blatant breaches of the right to life and protection and, even if a state allows for push-backs in the legislation, the commission stays silent? so, when will you finally act to ensure protection for all refugees in the european union? bring them to court like you did with hungary. make border and asylum fronts conditional. speak up and demand respect for human rights, and use the current wave of solidarity to push for a common responsibility of all member states for our asylum seekers.
